# Division XII: General Liability, S&W, UEBTF, SIBTF & Exclusive Remedy

> **Governing Scope:** Chapters 98–115 | Active California Workers' Compensation Practice Reference

---


## Chapter 98: Vocational Rehabilitation

For injuries occurring on or after 1/1/04, vocational rehabilitation
benefits have been repealed. Basically there is no more vocational
rehabilitation benefit except for a nontransferable voucher for a state
approved or accredited school.

LC§139.5 (repealed 1/1/09)

Repeal of Section 139.5 terminated right to VR not finally ordered
before 2009. Absent a savings clause, all non-vested rights
terminated.

Weiner v. Ralph's Company (2009) 74 CCC
736, 37 CWCR 147 (WCAB - en banc)

& Weiner v. Ralph's (2009) 74 CCC 958, 37
CWCR 205 (WCAB en banc denying
reconsideration). Writ denied: Weiner v.
WCAB (2010) 75 CCC 303 (CA 2d- wd)

Another decision affirming the demise of 139.5, this time annulling
an award of retro VRMA/VRTD because on 01/01/09, the case was
pending before CA on a petition for writ of review and thus not
finally determined

Beverly Hilton Hotel v. WCAB (Boganim)
(2009) 74 CCC 927, 37 CWCR 189 (CA 2" —
pub)

Portion of retro VRMA awarded but which was subject of recon
petition not payable because issue did not become final before repeal
LC 139.5.

L.A. County Fire v. WCAB (Norton) (2010) 38
CWCR 107 (CA 2™ — pub)

Award of retro VRMA on 10/07/08, followed by denial of recon on
12/11/08 did not vest because defendant’s right to appeal had not
expired before 01/01/09

Sanchez v. WCAB (2010) 75 CCC 786 (CA en
—wd)

RU’s 12/24/08 determination awarding VR benefits was not final
order and had not vested prior to 01/01/09 repeal of VR; defendant
did file an appeal of the determination on 01/15/09

Williams v. WCAB (2010) 75 CCC 797 (CA
1*— wd)

Unappealed VR order of 12/29/08 is enforceable because without an
appeal, order became final

Colleran v. WCAB (2010) 75 CCC 1322, 39
CWCR 7 (CA 2" — nonpub)

Timely notice of appeal of Rehab unit decision was effective
without filing D/R; thus defendant=s appeal should be heard.

Kroger Co v. WCAB (Velasquez) (2012) 77
CCC 495 (CA 2d-nonpub). See also Kroger v.
W.C.A.B. (Rodriguez) (2012) 77 CCC 945
(CA 2d - wd)

SUPPLEMENTAL JOB DISPLACEMENT VOUCHER

SB 863 created a somewhat different benefit pursuant to LC Section
4658.7 and amended the prior voucher provisions to some extent.
The current SJDB section applies to injuries occurring on or after
January 1, 2004 and before January 1, 2013. The new benefit

| pursuant to LC Section 4658.7 is described below

LC §4658.5(a), effective 01/01/2013
For emergency regs, see 10116.9 — 10118 &
10133.31 thru 10133.60

Per SB 863, vouchers issued on or after January 1, 2013 expire two
years after furnished to the employee or five years after the date of
injury, whichever is later.

LC §4658.5(d)

Subsequent injuries: Per SB 863, The employer is not responsible
for compensation for injuries incurred while using the voucher.

LC §4658.5(e)

The amount of the voucher depends on the disability rating: less than
15% gets up to $4,000; 15-25% gets up to $6,000; 26-49% gets up to
$8,000; 50-99% gets up to $10,000.

LC§4658.5(a)

The money can be used for tuition, books and other expenses but no
more than 10% can be used for vocational counseling.

LC§4658.5(b)

Within 10 days of the last payment of temporary disability the
employer is to send the injured worker by certified mail a notice of
| potential voucher rights.

LC§4658.5(c); R§10133.51(b); use Form
DWC-AD 10133.52

Disputes:

Any party may request that the AD resolve a dispute regarding any
aspect of the job displacement benefit. The party must complete
Form DWC-AD 10133.55 and comply with the procedural
requirements and time limits set forth in Rule 10133.54

R§§10133.54 & 10133.55

New Voucher rules per SB 863: The amended SJDB section
| applies to injuries occurring on or after January I , 2013.

LC §4658.7(a)

Eligibility under amended program: The worker is entitled to the
voucher unless within 60 days of the employer's receipt of the initial
permanent and stationary report finding permanent disability, the
employer makes an offer of regular or alternative/ modified work
lasting at least 12 months. If the physician is provided a job
description, the physician must evaluate work capabilities and
activity restrictions, and the adjuster must forward this information
to the employer.

LC§4658.7(b)

Timing of amended voucher: The voucher must be offered within
20 days of expiration of the time limit for the employer to make the

VOCATIONAL REHABILITATION
LC §4658.7(c)

Amount and use of amended voucher: A voucher of up to $6,000
can be used only at a public school or with a provider on the state's
Eligible Training Provider list, and for other uses including licensing
fees, vocational counseling (only up to 10% of the voucher), tools,
or a computer (only up to $1,000).

LC §4658.7(d) & (e)

Expiration of amended voucher: Vouchers issued after January 1,
2013 expire two years after issuance or five years after date of
injury, whichever is later.

LC §4658.7(f)

Settlement of amended voucher: Settlement or commutation of the
SJDB is not permitted.

LC §4658.7(g)

BUT: C&R in 2014 injury case may include settlement of voucher if
there is good faith dispute regarding injury.

Beltran v. Structural Steel Fabricators (2016)
81 CCC 1224, 44 CWCR 185 (WCAB)

Employer liability: The employer is not responsible for injuries
incurred while using either the existing or the amended voucher.

LC §4658.5(e) & LC §4658.7(i)

Injured worker may be entitled to 2 (or more) vouchers: Pre SB

Unreasonable delay in issuance of voucher justified 25% penalty,

actually used. [Presumably, said penalty will have to be paid

Garcia v. Sara Lee Corporation (2016) 44
CWCR 251 (WCAB)

Penalty for failure to timely provide job displacement voucher is
limited to penalty on the amount of the voucher actually used by
applicant, not on the gross amount of the voucher.

Sandoval v. La Quinta Inn and Suites (2016)
82 CCC 114 (WCAB)

PRE 01/01/02004 VR:

Timely filed rehab appeal lodged w/Board but not RU, conferred
jurisdiction on the Board, and filing appeal under wrong case
number was technical breach which did not warrant denial of
petition.

Enoch v. WCAB (2006) 34 CWCR 182, 71
CCC 904 (CA 5" - non pub)

VRMA not subject to credit for earnings in other employment (a 2m
| job held by I/W pre-injury)

Gamble v. WCAB_ (2006) 34 CWCR 254, 71
CCC 1015 (CA 4" - pub)

CA denied credit against VRMA benefits for post-injury wages.
VRMA is not a wage replacement benefit.

Medrano v. WCAB (2008) 73 CCC 1407, 36
CWCR 219 (CA 2d pub)

Defendant is not entitled to credit against VRMA for wages earned
in a post-injury job with a different ER, applying both Gamble and
Medrano. VRMA is not a wage replacement benefit, said the court.

Galvao v. WCAB (2008) 73 CCC 1639, 37
CWCR 5 (CA 1*- nonpub)

Initial VR request timely if made within 1 year of last funding of
PD, even if last funding of PD made pursuant a petition to reopen
which was made more than 5 years after the DOI.

Fresno Unified School Dist. v. WCAB (2006)
34 CWCR 290 (CA 5" - non pub)

VRMA due at TD rate outside cap where delay caused by
Employer’s dispute w/VR vendor in other cases.

Paramount Farms v. WCAB (Velasquez)
(2006) 34 CWCR 293 (CA 5"- non pub)

Where VR interrupted, five years from DOI runs, defendant extends
time by letter that stated deadline to contact defendant to restart VR,
applicant fails to request services until after said deadline, defendant
is not required to provide services.

Adams v. WCAB (2005) 70 CCC 881 (CA a
- wd)

XV. CASE END


## Chapter 99: Case End

Payment of Award: When Stipulated Award stated that indemnity was
payable at $170/week beginning “forthwith” in the total sum of $48,000,
defendant interpreted the agreement to mean that payments were to be
made in weekly installment but the WCAB construed term “forthwith”
to require immediate payment of entire lump sum as contemplated by
applicant and found agreement to be unambiguous in its terms. The
WCAB also affirmed the WCJ’s finding of violation of LC Section
5814

The May Department Stores Company v.
WCAB (Kashafi) (2005) 70 CCC 509
(wd)

Check that attorney’s fees have been paid

Check that all liens have been paid.

In computer transfer case to “CLOSED”. Print “Closed” sheet.

Calendar petition to reopen or VR period if applicable

Can get attorney’s fee for enforcement of award.

LC5814.5

Applicant’s attorney may consider petitioning to be relieved as attorney
of record to prevent client from continuing to telephone with small
problems, and to not be responsible for failing to reopen, and to start the
statute of limitation running on any possible malpractice. Statute is
tolled while attorney still represents client. Can also send client a closing
letter thanking them and saying that attorney does not represent them
anymore with optional paragraph that if he or she needs legal
representation in the future to call the attorney.

CCP§340.6; Lockley v. Law Office 66
CCC 1049 (SOL is tolled during period
attorney continues to represent client)

Reduce physical file and transfer to storage. Consider putting file on CD.

LC Section 4900 prohibits assignment of a workers comp award even if
such award is converted to a civil judgment.

Matthews v. Liberty Assignment Corp
(2016) 44CWCR 105, 81 CCC 415 (CA
5""_ pub)

XVI. LIABILITY


## Chapter 100: General Liability Principles

Workers’ compensation laws must be liberally construed in the
employee’s favor, and all reasonable doubts as to whether an
injury arose out of employment are to be resolved in favor of the
employee.

Garza v. WCAB 3 C3d 312, 90 CR 355, 35 CCC
500

Liability is imposed without regard to negligence.

LC§3600(a)(3), Cal. Const. Art. XTV, §4

Death on job found industrial based on neutral risk doctrine
because decedent died from mysterious or criminal
circumstances.

Jetro Cash and Carry Holdings v. WCAB (2008)
98 (CA 2" - wd)

Jurisdiction

There is no requirement that a worker spend a majority or any
other specific portion of his working time in California to be
“regularly employed” in California,

LC§3600.5(a); Carpenter v. John Christner
Trucking, Inc. (1997) 62 CCC 979, 25 CWCR 72
(Driver spent 32% of time in Cal.)

WCAB has subject matter jurisdiction where contract for hire is
made even though injury occurred outside Cal. If acceptance of
employment is by phone, place is of acceptance controls.

Janzen v. WCAB (1997) 61 CA4th 109, 62 CCC
9, 26 CWCR 8; Bowen v. WCAB (1999) 73
CA4th 15, 64 CCC 745, 27 CWCR 153 (baseball
player signed contract in Cal even though
approved in other state.)

Defendant Canadian Corporation had sufficient contact with
California to confer WCAB jurisdiction (see case for details).

Oz Optics v. WCAB (Ghassemi) (2005) 70 CCC
1361 (CA 6" - wd)

California jurisdiction found even though contract signed in
Indiana because terms agreed to by phone with agent in
California. Choice of forum contract clause invalid under LC
Section 5000 because it was an attempt to avoid California
jurisdiction.

Tampa Bay Devil Rays v. WCAB (2008) 73 CCC
550 (CA 4" - wd)

No California jurisdiction over flight attendant’s injury in Ohio
because contract of hire not made in California and applicant not
regularly employed in California.

Thibodeau v. WCAB (2008) 73 CCC 553 (CA 2™
- wd)

California contact of hire, and thus California jurisdiction, made
when worker accepted job offer by phone when in California.

Cooper v. Roadmaster (2013) 41 CWCR 224
(WCAB)

Because applicant played 3 games in California while employed
by Tampa Bay, California has jurisdiction.

Tampa Bay Buccaneers v. WCAB (Curry) (2008)
73 CCC 944 (CA 4"" - wd)

A non-resident football player hired elsewhere is exempted from
California w/c law while temporarily in California if the
conditions specified in LC Section 3600.5(b) have been met

Carroll v. Louisiana Workers Comp.
Corporation (2010) 38 CWCR 177 (WCAB)

WCAB has jurisdiction of claim of pro football player who orally
accepted contract offer in California but signed contract in
Florida, per LC Sections 3600.5 and 5305

Brache v. Tampa Bay Storm (2010) 38 CWCR
239 (WCAB)

WCAB had jurisdiction regarding injuries with Oakland Raiders
and first two years with Houston Texans because that contract
was entered into in California. Injuries in subsequent
employments were not subject to California jurisdiction because
no California residence at the time and contracts not entered into
in California

Joane v. Great Divide Ins. Co. (2010) 38 CWCR
242 (WCAB)

Playing 13 games in California over a 19 year career in NFL did
not constitute sufficient contracts with California to justify
California jurisdiction.

Matthews v. NFL (2012) 77 CCC 722 (CA 2d-
wd)

No California jurisdiction over NFL player on Arizona team
because of reasonable mandatory forum selection clause in
employment contract and limited connection to California

| regarding claimed cumulative injury.

McKinley v. Travelers Indemnity Co. (2013) 78
CCC 23, 41 CWCR 7 (WCAB - en banc).
McKinley v. WCAB (2013) 78 CCC 872 (CA4th-
wd)

| legislation.

Pro athletes CT claims limited: California jurisdiction over

Labor Code Section 3600.5, effective
01/01/2014

Indian Tribes:
Tribal sovereign immunity extends to nontribal employee of
commercial activity (medical clinic) outside tribal land.

Shingle Springs Band of Miwok Indians v.
WCAB 66 CCC 1283

WCAB and CA conclude that WCJ must take evidence re waiver

Indian tribe and the State of California. [distinguishing
Middletown Rancheria of Pomo Indians, 63 CCC 15.]

Tribal First v. WCAB (Fales-Brown) (2005) 70
CCC 922 (CA 1* -wd)

If employer wants to make special appearance to avoid general
appearance, it should appear only to contest personal jurisdiction
and not contest other issues.

Janzen v. WCAB (1997) 61 CA4th 109, 62 CCC
9, 26 CWCR 8

No WCAB jurisdiction over 132a v. Indian tribe b/c no waiver of

Sullivan v. WCAB (2006) 71 CCC 1065 (CA 5®-
non pub)

No waiver of sovereign immunity by Indian tribe. Even if tribe
violated Tribal Gaming Compact, injured worker had no standing
to enforce it, as only the State of California may do so.

Keitt v. WCAB (2012) 77 CCC 455 (CA 4" —wd)

Arising Out of Employment and in the Course of
Employment

No liability unless injury AOE-COE

Arising out of employment and occurring in the course of
employment.

|
LC§3600; Maher v. WCAB 48 CCC 326

Mason v. Lake Group, LLC 69 CCC 353 (Pool
tech went down water slide before park opened —

Clerk suffered injury AOE/COE as a result of gunshot wound

Super Mercado Mexico v WCAB (Nunez) (2006)
71 CCC 103 (CA 6" - wd)

Verbal dispute between co-employees on freeway on way to

found to be AOF/COE because injury resulted from “mixed”

| personal and employment risk.

TimeWarner Co. v. WCAB, (2004) 69 CCC 1610
(wd)

Retail worker’s injury while retrieving personally purchased item
from different department at end of work shift is compensable
because of dual purpose doctrine.[See Price v. WCAB (1984) 13
CWCR 1, 49 CCC 742]

Costco Wholesale v. WCAB (Slayton) (2009) 74
CCC 319, 37 CWCR 65 (CA 5" - nonpub)

Murder of employee at workplace by former lover not
compensable as employment merely provided a stage for the
homicide.

Wright v. SCIF (2013) 41 CWCR 199 (WCAB)

Heart attack at home as the result of telephone call from
employer was held not in the course of employment.

Ralphs Grocery Co. v. WCAB (Moeller) (1997)
58 CA4th 647, 62 CCC 1399, 25 CWCR 293
(ed. note says case should be decertified at the
very least; applicant suffered heart attack at
home having been off work for 7 months due to
finger injury.)

Death from ruptured aneurysm after argument w/ supervisor was
non-industrial because medical evidence indicated only that there
was a possible connection between the argument and the
aneurysm, rather than a probable one.

A. Teichert and Sons, Inc. v. WCAB (Barron)
(2008) 73 CCC 1621, 37 CWCR 4 (CA 3-
nonpub)

Death caused by allergic reaction to peanuts eaten at company
party, on premises during work hours, found compensable

Bank of America v. WCAB (Quijano) (2010) 75
CCC 1012 (CA 2"!— wd)

Not on employer’s premises. Question is: Was the employee
engaged in activity that was expressly or impliedly authorized by
the employer.

Schneider(Willick) v. Reliance Nat'l Ins. Co.
(2002) (wd) 67 CCC 1160, 30 CWCR 249
(Stanford professor on sabbatical hit by auto
while working on laptop at Starbuck’s in New
Jersey within AOE-COE)

Ifa disability is precipitated by a movement incidental to
employment, the injury is compensable even though the
movement is normal. If, on the other hand, the injury is wholly
spontaneous, it is not compensable just because it occurs on the
job. If there is a positive showing that the sole cause of the injury
is an inherent defect of the employee it is not compensable.

Allied Signal, Inc. v. WCAB (Briggs) 66 CCC
1333 (used urinal and turned to sink to wash
hands felt pain in back. Personal comfort
doctrine is COE — normal movement not due to
inherent defect is AOE. See cases cited.)

Reversing the WCJ’s decision, the WCAB found that applicant’s
fall, which was caused by his non-industrial seizure disorder,
caused an injury of striking his body against the wall, scaffolding
and ground and thus the injury occurred in the course of his
employment. The WCAB rejected the WCJ’s contention that the
new Labor Code Sections 4663 and 4664 would compel a
different result, concluding that said sections did not affect Labor
Code Section 3600 and 3208.3 governing determinations of
whether an injury is AOE/COE.

Rayes v. Hart Plastering (2005) 70 CCC 223
(WCAB- significant panel decision)

20 (CA 4" pub)

No employer liability where injury:

LC§3600

1. Caused by intoxication or unlawful use of drugs

LC§3600(a)(4), 60 CCC 117, 59 CCC 1077,
Mintz v. Chubb/Pacific Indem. Ins. Co. (1995)

(Burden is on defense to show that intoxication

Initial physical aggressor defense not applicable in case of

(2011) 76 CCC 596 (CA 2d- wd)

## Chapter 102: Traveling To Medical Treatment Or
EXAMINATION

Injury received by an employee while en route to medical treatment
for an industrial injury is compensable.

Laines v. WCAB (1975) 40 CCC 365

An injury is not compensable if the employee materially deviates
from a reasonably direct route for an extraneous purpose, such as a
personal errand.

Trejo v. Maciel 31 CCC 462; Phillips v.
WCAB 57 CCC 243 (Didn’t take direct route
to Sacrament from Loyalton.)

But once the deviation is completed and the employee is traveling a
direct route to treatment facility the deviation has ended.

68 CCC 469

Injury in MVA at beginning of long drive (140 miles) from
relative’s house to PTP’s office not compensable because beyond
reasonable geographic area. [The PTP’s office was only a few miles
from app’s home]

CWCR 245 (CA 4""— pub)


## Chapter 103: Traveling For Business - Commercial
TRAVELER

H§4.117

Commercial Traveler. All activities of the travel are considered part
of employment, including obtaining food and lodging, except for
purely personal activities that bear no relationship to the travel or
employment activity. Some latitude is allowed. Reasonably
foreseeable social and recreational activities are covered such as
going to museums or renting a moped.

Cal Cas Indem. Exch v. IAC 1 CCC 80;
Dalgleish v. Holt 108 CA2d 561; IBM.

(Hill) (2002) 67 CCC 1386; 30 CWCR 302
(auto accident while on a sightseeing trip to
cave park while on layover in Puerto Rico;
court said Dahlberg, supra, was wrongly
decided)

Purely personal activities are those activities that are not impliedly
authorized.

43 CCC 161; Dimmig v. WCAB 37 CCC 211,

## Chapter 105: General / Special Employer / Co-Employer

A general employer hires and pays the wages of an employee. The —
employee is then loaned to a special employer. The employee is
employed by the general employer but the special employer has the
| right to control or does control the worker.

Riley v. Southwest Marine, Inc. (1988) 203 -
CA3d 1242, 53 CCC 502

The liability of the general and special employers for compensation
benefits is joint and several. But the insurer for the general pays the
claim.

916; Fireman’s Fund Indem. Co. v. SCIF
(Smith) 93 CA2d 408; Advising Cal.
Employers CEB §4.42; Ins. Code §11663

BUT

On rehearing, the Court of Appeal has reversed the decision it issued
in a published opinion in 10/2004, (Miceli 69 CCC 1207). The
question was whether CIGA was liable when there were insured
general and special employers, but the general employer’s insurer
became insolvent. In its prior opinion, the court held that the special
employer’s carrier was “other insurance”, thus eliminating CIGA
liability. On rehearing, the court held that it was not necessary for a
form endorsement excluding coverage for special employees to be
completed in order for the special employer to avoid liability. Based
on the evidence, the court concluded that the special employer’s
insurer had no coverage, and in absence of such “other insurance”,
CIGA is liable

UABILITY
General Cas. Ins. Co. v. WCAB (Miceli)
(2005) 70 CCC 953, 33 CWCR 207 (CA 2nd-
pub)

BUT, Ina decision on remittitur, the WCAB followed the
unpublished Court of Appeals decision in Miceli, but declared that
the decision is not binding in other similar cases and that each case
must be decided upon its own facts.

Miceli y. American Home Assurance
Company (2006) 34 CWCR 124, 71 CCC 599
(WCAB)

Therefore: Award of compensation to be paid by CIGA cannot be

parties was not established and a specific finding regarding the
existence of “other insurance” is needed.

106. PENALTIES AND 5814 INCREASE

Applies to All Dates of Injuries. The SB 899 provisions regarding
penalties apply to all dates of injury.

LC §5814(h); Abney v. AERA Energy (2004)
69 CCC 1552 (en banc) (the new 5814 applies
to all unreasonable delays regardless of date
of injury); writ denied 70 CCC 460 SCIF v.

United Airlines v. WCAB (Conner) (2005) 70

The CA held that the SB 899 changes to section 5814 apply
retroactively to all injuries and cases pending at the time of the
amendment’s effective date (06/01/04). However, the court also
held that the changes do not affect the ability to seek multiple
penalties for separate and distinct delays.

2™ pub). See also United Airlines v. WCAB

Appeal followed Abney.

“Upon the approval of a compromise and release, findings and

Bad faith actions or tactics that are frivolous or solely intended to
cause unnecessary delay.

LC§5813

To get such sanctions a party must file a petition. Offending party
must be given notice and an opportunity to be heard (due process).

WCAB (Bradley) (2005) 133 CWCR 155 (CA

general fund.

A bad faith action or tactic is one which results from a willful failure
to comply with a statutory or regulatory obligation or from a willful
intent to disrupt or delay the proceedings of the Workers'
Compensation Appeals Board.

A frivolous bad faith action or tactic is one that is done for an
improper motive or is indisputably without merit.

Violations subject to the provisions of Labor Code Section 5813
shall include but are not limited to the following:

Failure to appear or appearing late at a conference or hearing shall
be deemed a bad faith action or tactic solely intended to cause
unnecessary delay where a reasonable excuse is not offered or the
offending party has demonstrated a pattern of such conduct.

Filing a pleading, petition or legal document shall be deemed a bad
faith action or tactic which is frivolous or solely intended to cause
unnecessary delay unless there is some reasonable justification for
filing such a document.

Failing to comply with the Workers' Compensation Appeals Board's
Rules of Practice and Procedure or an order of the Workers’
Compensation Appeals Board, including an order of discovery,
shall be deemed a bad faith action or tactic which is frivolous or
solely intended to cause unnecessary delay unless such failure
results from mistake, inadvertence, surprise, or excusable neglect.

Melton v. Industrial Indemnity Company 66
CCC 41 (long opinion)

Malicious prosecution against Applicant/Applicant attorney.
The CA affirmed trial court’s holding that applicant’s attorneys
were not liable on malicious prosecution theory for bringing 132a
claim. The CA concluded that the applicant attorney were entitled
to rely upon the information they possessed at the time of filing of
the 132a petition , and based upon that information, they had
probable cause to file the petition and thus did not engage in
malicious prosecution. The CA notes that failure of an attorney to
conduct a reasonable investigation is not relevant to the

Serious and willful misconduct is something much more than mere
negligence, or even gross or culpable negligence ~ it involves
conduct of a quasi criminal nature, the intentional doing of
something either with the knowledge that it is likely to result in
serious injury or with a wanton and reckless disregard of its possible
consequences. The mere failure to perform a statutory duty is not,
standing alone, willful misconduct. To constitute willful
misconduct, “there must be actual knowledge or what the law deems
to be the equivalent of actual knowledge.”

Bekins Moving & Storage Co. v. WCAB
(Garner) 45 CCC 256; Frederickson v. WCAB
66 CCC 1554 (wd)

reckless and absolute disregard of its possible damaging
consequences.”

Willful misconduct implies the intentional doing of something with
either the knowledge that serious injury is probable, as distinguished
from possible, or with a wanton and reckless disregard of the
possible result. Anything less is not serious and willful misconduct.

requires an intentional act or failure to act.

OTHER CONSIDERATIONS AT WCAB
CIGA v. WCAB (Weitzman) (2005) 70 CCC
556, 33 CWCR 95 (CA 2™-pub)

On rehearing, the Court of Appeal has reversed the decision it issued
in a published opinion in 10/2004, (Miceli 69 CCC 1207). The
question was whether CIGA was liable when there were insured
general and special employers, but the general employer’s insurer
became insolvent. In its prior opinion, the court held that the special
employer’s carrier was “other insurance”, thus eliminating CIGA.
liability. On rehearing, the court held that it was not necessary fora
form endorsement excluding coverage for special employees to be
completed in order for the special employer to avoid liability. Based
on the evidence, the court concluded that the special employer’s
insurer had no coverage, and in absence of such “other insurance”,
CIGA is liable.

UEBTF can petition to have “parent” or substantial shareholder

Substantial shareholder is on who owns at least 15% of the

UEBTF is authorized to determine who is parent or substantial

Bankruptcy (BK)

An award against an employer is dischargeable by employer in
bankruptcy. The applicant cannot enforce the award against the
personal assets of the employer. (An order by the bankruptcy court
that applicant is relieved from the injunction for the purpose of
proceeding in the WCAB does not change the enforceability of the
award by applicant against the employer.)

It should not be necessary for applicant to get relief from the
automatic stay of a BK. As long as applicant is merely attempting to
establish liability as a prerequisite to recovering from the UEBTF
relief from the injunction or the reopening of the BK case should not
be necessary. But if the WCAB won’t proceed it will be necessary
to get relief.

Slali v. Ruiz 67 CCC 634 (good case to read)
(closed BK can be reopened to get relief from
stay.)

The debt owed to UEBTF by an uninsured employer because
UEBTF paid a WCAB award against an employer is dischargeable
by the employer in bankruptcy. It is not an “excise tax.”

George v. UEF (3/18/04) 69 CCC 239, 32
CWCR 83 (distinguishing In re Camilli)

Ifa notice of bankruptcy of the employer is received must do
additional steps. Do these steps even if employee was not named as
a creditor or the steps may not be necessary. CYA

Enquirer No. 8, p. 16 (2/95)

Bankruptcy of self-insured ER is not basis for vacating order

Center (2010) 38 CWCR 217 (WCAB)

approving C&R; award is payable by self-insured security fund

## Chapter 111: Subsequent Injuries Benefits Trust
FUND

LC§4751, R§10940, 10942, 10946; CEB
§13.119

Limitation Period

There is no specified statute of limitations but case law has
established limitations.

If the application for SIF benefits is filed with the application for
adjudication then the application for SIF benefits is not barred if the
application for adjudication is not barred.

LC§5405, 5410

If the application for SIF benefits is not filed with the application for
adjudication
1. it must be filed within one year of the last furnishing of
voluntary disability payments or medical treatment, or
2. within five years of the date of injury, or
3. (a) within a reasonable time after the expiration of (1) and
(2) above and (b) applicant did not know and could not have
reasonably been deemed to know that there was a
substantial likelihood that she or he would have been
entitled to subsequent injury fund benefits

SIF v. WCAB (Talcott) (1970) 35 CCC 80
(Supreme Court); SIF v. WCAB (Woodburn)
(1970) (Supreme Court) (Returned to WCAB
to determine reasonableness of delay in
filing.)

If the five years from the DOI is about to expire and applicant
knows or should reasonable know that there is a substantial
likelihood that he or she would be entitled to SIF benefits, an
application for SIF benefits should be filed within a reasonable time.

and occupation.

code means 100% disability), or

Calculation Steps

Steps to calculate SIF liability. Note that the calculation is done in
weeks and not percentage of disability because percentage disability
is progressive. It is not computed like apportionment.

Medical treatment is not considered in the calculations.

Step 1. Total Disability Weeks. Determine total over-all disability
due to the combined adjusted rating for the subsequent industrial
disability and pre-existing disability in weeks. Use the date of
injury of the industrial injury and the adjusted total disability
percentage to determine the number of weeks and weekly rate. The
ending date of payments is the P&S date plus the number of total

weeks of disability. This ending date remains fixed.

Total weeks, Total rate, Total end date

© 2018 Med-Legal, LLC www.GetRecords.com 800-244-3495


<!-- PAGE 426 -->
QUICK REFERENCE

Step 2. Industrial ity Weeks. Determine the number of
weeks attributed solely to the subsequent industrial permanent
disability using the adjusted rating and date of injury for the
subsequent industrial injury. The industrial permanent disability
payments would commence after the P&S date and end after the
number of weeks have expired. (This is computed based upon the
disability rating and not a C&R settlement value.)

OTHER CONSIDERATIONS AT WCAB
LC§4751
LC§4753 (last sentence)
Ind weeks, Ind rate, Ind end date

Step 3. SCIF Start and End Date. SCIF weeks of payment is
determine by subtracting Step 2 from Step 1. SCIF payments
commence after the industrial payments end. The start date is the
P&S date plus the weeks determined in step 2 and the ending date is
the date determined in Step 1. In other words, the subsequent
industrial disability delays the start of SCIF payments but does not
affect the ending date.

1. From P&S date to Ind end date SCIF to pay
Total rate — Ind rate.

2. From Ind end date to Total end date SCIF
to pay Total rate — Ind LP rate.

3. From Total end date SCIF to pay Total LP
rate — Ind LP rate.

Step 4. Life Pension Payments. If the total disability determined in
step 1 results in a life pension, the life pension payments are to
begin when the permanent disability weeks end as determined in
step 1. If the subsequent disability determined in step 2 results in
life pension payments then the life pension payments in step | are

SIF LP rate ‘
Total LP rate — Ind LP rate — Credit LP rate

reduced by the life pension payments in step 2.
Step 5. On-going Weekly Payments Credit. If credit payments
are made after the P&S date

1, From P&S date to Ind end date SIF to pay
Total rate — Ind rate — Credit rate

2. From Ind end date to Total end date SIF to
pay Total rate — Ind LP rate — Credit rate

3. From Total end date SIF to pay Total LP
tate — Ind LP rate — Credit rate

Step 6. Benefit-Credit. If disability payments have been received
for any of the pre-existing disabilities (e.g. social security disability
payments, other workers’ compensation cases), determine the
amount received less attorney’s fee and cost for obtaining the
benefit. Divide the net amount by the weekly rate for the
subsequent industrial disability to obtain a number of week’s
reduction. (Social security credit can eat up the entire SIF benefit.)

LC§4753, H§8.09[4]

Step 7. SIF Attorney-Fee-Credit. If an attorney’s fee is awarded
for obtaining SIF benefits, the start date is further delayed and the
number of weeks of payments is reduced accordingly. Divide the
attorney’s fee by the weekly rate to obtain the number of weeks
reduction.

Credit ‘ Benefit — Benefit attorney’s fee +
SIF attorney’s fee

Step 8. Credit Delayed Start Date. SCIF payments are further
delayed from the starting date determined in step 3 by the number of
weeks determined in steps 4 and 5. Permanent disability payments
then commence and continue to the same ending date determined in
step 1. The number of weeks SCIF pays is Step 3 weeks minus the
weeks determined in Steps 4 and 5.

SIF weeks ‘
Total weeks — Ind weeks — Credit weeks

Credit weeks ‘ Credit amount / Total rate

It is unlawful to make or present any knowingly false or fraudulent
material statement for the purpose of obtaining or denying any
compensation.

or in opposition to, any claim for compensation for the purpose of
obtaining or denying any compensation ...””

It is unlawful to make any knowingly false statement with intent to

Any person convicted of WC fraud shall be ineligible to receive or
retain compensation. Not all benefits will be denied. Only those
benefits obtained as a result of false statements. Tensfeldt
analysis:(1) an otherwise compensable injury, (2) substantial
medical evidence supporting an award of compensation not
stemming from the misrepresentation, and (3) credibility not so
destroyed as to make claimant unbelievable as to any disputed issue.

## Chapter 115: Exclusive Remedy

The exclusive remedy of an injured employee against his or her
employer for a work-related injury is to make a claim in the WCAB.
Injured worker cannot sue his or her employer.

LC§3602(a); Lenk v. Total-Western, Inc. 66
CCC 711; Wright v. Beverly Fabrics, Inc. 67
CCC 51 (helping coworker on day off is
incident to employment — half million dollar
verdict thrown out); Saldana v. Globe-Weiss
Systems Co. (1991) 56 CCC 577

An exception to exclusivity is where the employer is uninsured for
workers’ compensation or is not legally self-insured.

LC§3706; Hernandez v. Chavez Roofing, Inc.
235 CA3d 1092; 56 CCC 650; LC§3700(b)

Another exception is fraudulent concealment of industrial source of
injury. The essential elements are (1) the employer knew that the
plaintiff had suffered a work-related injury before the employee
knew; (2) the employer concealed that knowledge from the plaintiff;
and (3) the injury was aggravated as a result of such concealment.

LC§3602(b)(2); Forster v. Zerox Corp. 40
Cal.3d 306, 13 CWCR 304; Palestini v.
General Dynamics Corp. 67 CCC 754, 30
CWCR 172; Jensen v. Amgen, Inc. (2003) 105
CA4th 1322, 68 CCC 196, 31 CWCR 24;
Encinas v. Catholic Healthcare West 68 CCC
184 (np)

The exclusivity rule can apply to claims for purely emotional
distress, as well as to claims for physical injury.

Livitsanos v. Superior Court 57 CCC 355

The exclusivity rule does not apply when the employer’s conduct
falls outside the compensation bargain, e.g. racial and sexual
harassment, involuntary confinement, violent and coercive criminal
conduct, fraud for concealment of workplace danger.

Fermino v. Fedco 59 CCC 296; Shoemaker v.
Myers 55 CCC 494; Vacanti v. SCIF 24 C4th
800, 65 CCC 1402; Fretland v. Co. of
Humboldt 64 CCC 195; Fotiades v. Hi-Tech
Collision & Painting Services, Inc. 67 CCC
255 (np) (photo in restroom - $1.5 M reduced.
No ratification.);

The employer can still be sued even though compensation was paid
| by another entity.

Huffman v. City of Poway 65 CCC 1280

The damages must be due to personal injury or death and actions for
defamation.

Vacanti v. SCIF 24 C4th 800, 65 CCC 1402;
Davaris v. Cubaleski (1993) 12 CA4th 1583;
Livitsanos v. Sup. Court 2 C4th 744

Employee accused of theft. Jury found employer’s conduct was
outrageous but appellate court held that investigations of employee
theft are within the workers’ compensation exclusivity provisions as
long as the investigation does not constitute false imprisonment or
other violent or coercive acts against the employee.

Palacio v. Longs Drug Stores California, Inc.
68 CCC 1673 (np); see also Livitsanos v.
Superior Court (1992) 57 CCC 355

Arbitrator’s award of reinstatement with back pay was not subject to
WIC exclusive remedy rule because award was on issues not
encompassed within the “compensation bargain”. Clause inserted in
court order barring further W/C benefits stricken as exceeding the
authority of the trial judge

Sunline Transit Agency v. ATU local 1277
(2010) 75 CCC 1155, 38 CWCR 281 (CA 4"
—pub)

Injury after unlawful demotion

In order to be exempt from the exclusive remedy provisions the
unlawful demotion must be a substantial factor in causing the
subsequent injury. E.g. disability discrimination and demotion to a
more arduous position that aggravates and causes further disability
is not within workers compensation whereas demotion due to age
discrimination and employee is hit by a bus while working at the
new position would be in workers compensation.

Huffinan v. Interstate Brands Companies 69
CCC 956, 32 CWCR 232

Malicious Prosecution. Employer can sue insurance company for
malicious prosecution where insurance company sues employer for
additional premium without probable cause. Elements of malicious
prosecution are: (1) action terminated in employer’s favor, (2) was
brought without probable cause, and (3) was initiated with malice.
Insurance company lawyers can also be sued where insurance
company claims it relied upon the advice of its lawyers. Even if one
of several claims is without probable cause a malicious prosecution
suit may be brought.

action may be maintained where most but not
all of the amount sought in the underlying

rejection form or send certified rejection letter.

Negligent Performance of an Undertaking. “One who undertakes,

may use Cal-OSHA provisions to show a duty or standard of care to
the same extent as any other regulation or statute, whether the
defendant is their employer or a third party. The lone exception
arises when the state is the defendant based on actions it took or
failed to take in its regulatory capacity; in such cases, Cal-OSHA
provisions remain inadmissible to show liability based on breach of
the statutory duty to inspect worksites and enforce safety rules.”
Cal-OSHA Safety regulations of title 8 can be used as presumption
of negligence under EC 669. Judge can take judicial notice of the
regulations under EC 452. OSHA employee cannot be an expert.
Whether a citation was or was not issued is not admissible.

LC§6304.5; Elsner v. Uveges (2004) 33
CWCR 19, 69 CCC 1511 (Supreme Court)
(not retroactive; in effect for conduct after

Non-negligent Hirer / Property Owner

OTHER CONSIDERATIONS NOT AT THE BOARD

The general rule is that hirers of independent contractors, i.e.
landowners, lessees, and general contractors are not liable for the
negligence of independent contractors who cause injury to others.

LC§3353; BAJI 13.20; Srithong v. Total
Investment Co. 23 CA4th 721, 28 CR2d 672

Nondelegable Duty Exception: A landowner or lessor has a
nondelegable duty to keep and maintain their property in a safe
condition. Where an IC negligently injures another the owner or
lessor are liable.

BAJI 13.22; Srithong v. Total Investment Co.
23 Cal.App.4"" 721 (IC repairing roof. Hot tar
falls on lessee) Also failure to maintain
elevator or water heater.

A land owner (or possessor) that hires an independent contractor to
work at the property can be liable for injury to that contractor’s
employee resulting from a dangerous condition only if (1) the owner
knew, or should have known, of a latent or concealed preexisting
hazardous condition, (2) the independent contractor did not know of
this condition and could not reasonably have discovered it, and (3)
the owner failed to warn the contractor about the condition.

Kinsman v. Unocal Corp. (2005) 70 CCC
1692, 34 CWCR 21 (Cal Supreme Court), &
Kinsman v. Unocal Corp. (2007) 72 CCC 970
(CA 1*-non pub)

Peculiar Risk Exception:

An exception to the general rule that the general contractor is not
liable for the negligence of an independent contractor is where the
work involves a peculiar risk, i.e. is inherently dangerous or ultra-
hazardous work. Then the general contractor is liable for damages to
those injured unless special precautions are taken.

BAJI 6.60, 13.21; See Zeiger v. State of Cal.
62 CCC 1424 for cites of other exceptions.

“Peculiar risk” defined: (it’s a dangerous activity) 1. Peculiar to
work (not unusual); 2. Arises out of character of work or place; 3.
General contractor should recognize the special danger or necessity
of taking special precautions.(e.g. digging a 12 foot deep trench)

BAJI 13.21.4, Rest. Torts 2d §416

A landlord is not responsible for injury to employee of tenant where
peculiar risk is caused solely by the tenant in connection with
tenant’s operations. The peculiar risk is a removable object, animal
or other instrumentality present on the land and not under control of
landowner.

Laico v. Chevron U.S.A., Inc. (2004) 33
CWCR 24, 69 CCC 1391 (CA 6'"-pub) (tenant
testing gasoline on land owned by defendant
where tenant’s employee exposed to benzene
that caused myelodysplasia.)

Hirer is Not Liable to Employee of Contractor Under Peculiar Risk
Exception

The employee of a contractor may not sue the hirer of the contractor
under either of the alternative version of the peculiar risk doctrine.
(1) a person who hires an independent contractor to do inherently
dangerous work, but who fails to provide in the contract or in some
other manner that special precaution be taken to avert the peculiar
risks of that work, can be liable if the contractor’s negligent
performance of the work causes injury to others. (2) Even if the
hirer has provided for special precaution in the contract or otherwise
, the hirer can nevertheless be liable if the contractor fails to exercise
reasonable care to take such precautions and the contractor’s
performance of the work causes injury to others. By public policy
workers’ compensation covers this situation.

Privette v. Superior Court 5 Cal.4" 689; 21
CR.2d 72; Toland v. Sunland Housing Group,
Inc. 18 Cal.4" 253; Warner v. General (1996)
24 CWCR 278; Voights v. Brutoco Eng. &
Constr. Co. 49 CA4th 354, 61 CCC 1027;
Redfeather v. Chevron USA Inc. (1997) 57
CA4th 702, 62 CCC 1075, 25 CWCR 253

The Court of Appeal applied Privette to an independent contractor
who was injured, and barred recovery from the hirer on the same
basis as an employee of the independent contractor would have been
barred.

Michael v. Denbeste Transportation, Inc.
(2006) 34 CWCR 83, 71 CCC 378 (CA 2” -
partial pub)

[Per the court, only 2 theories remain after
Privette: concealed hazard theory and hirer
exercised retained control that affirmatively
contributed to injury.]

Situations where Hirer Is Liable to Employee of Contractor

A hirer is liable to an employee of a contractor insofar as a hirer’s
exercise of retained control affirmatively contributes to the
employee’s injuries, e.g. directs an unsafe activity. Employee must
show direct negligence of the hirer by showing that the hirer
affirmatively contributed to the cause the injuries

Firefighter’s Rule

Firefighter’s Rule: publicly-employed firefighters and police
officers are barred from recovering from a third party if injury arose
from the act that prompted his/her to encounter the danger which led
to the injury.

(Probation officer injured in training by third
party training instructor. Lawsuit dismissed.)

A person who negligently starts a fire or creates a danger is not
liable to a firefighter or law enforcement officer who is injured
responding to the fire or incident.

OTHER CONSIDERATIONS NOT AT THE BOARD

Limitation period: “(a) An action against an attorney for a
wrongful act or omission, other than for actual fraud, arising in the
performance of professional services shall be commenced within
one year after the plaintiff discovers, or through the use of
reasonable diligence should have discovered, the facts constituting
the wrongful act or omission, or four years from the date of the
wrongful act or omission, whichever occurs first. In no event shall
the time for commencement of legal action exceed four years except
that the period shall be tolled during the time that any of the
following exist:

(1) The plaintiff has not sustained actual injury;

(2) The attorney continues to represent the plaintiff regarding the
specific subject matter in which the alleged wrongful act or
omission occurred;

(3) The attorney willfully conceals the facts constituting the
wrongful act or omission when such facts are known to the attorney,
except that this subdivision shall toll only the four-year limitation;
and

(4) The plaintiff is under a legal or physical disability which restricts

the plaintiff's ability to commence legal action.”

In an action by an injured worker against her self insured and self
adjusted employer for various activities arising out of her workers
comp case, the Court of Appeal concluded that plaintiff's claims for
fraudulent concealment of medical records and aggravation of
injuries, and for defamation for statements made to Clark’s medical
providers were barred b/c they involved protected activity
(communications arising from a judicial proceeding), and thus
granted defendants anti-SLAPP motion as to those two causes of
action. The Court of Appeal found that the claims for intentional
infliction of emotional distress and violations of the federal RICO
act were not barred by the SLAPP statute.

123. LIEN OR SUBROGATION AND DAMAGES in CIVIL CASE

The employer has a right to recover from an third party that has
caused or contributed to the employee’s injuries amounts the
employer has paid out in workers’ compensation benefits to the

employee.

The employer has three options:
(1) sue the third party directly LC §3852
(2) intervene in any lawsuit filed by or on behalf of the
injured employee against the third party LC §§3852,
3853
(3) file a lien on the employee’s judgment against the
third party. LC §3856(b)

Cal. Workers’ Damages Practice CEB §7.2;
Langley v. AM-PM Door, Inc. (np) 69 CCC
346 (Employer intervened. Jury instruction
told jury to award all damages to employee.
Jury awarded nothing to employer.
Employer should have asked for separated
verdict.)

Zurich North America v. WCAB (Nash)
(2013) 78 CCC 1247 (CA 4" - wd)

Steps Employer Should Take Before Termination
To Avoid 132a and FEHA Violation

An employer may have a policy of terminating an employee that has
a disability that prevents him or her from performing the essential
functions of the job after the employee has been off work for a
period of time (e.g. 6 months) as long as the policy is applied
equally to work related disabilities as to non-work related
disabilities and the policy is based upon the realities of business.

Savage v. Circuit City 32 CWCR 101

Employee has a work injury and, also, may have other non-
employment related disabilities that may interfere with the
performance of his or her job. At a minimum the employer should
take the following steps before the employee is terminated. The

| process should be carefully documented.

1, Is the employee’s employment disability permanent and
stationary?

Barns v. WCAB 54 CCC 433

2. Is there a job description stating the essential functions of the
job? The job description should be specific, for example, how much
and how often items are lifted and carried and how much each item
weighs, or how many minutes of continuous keyboarding are
permitted and how many minutes of total keyboarding are permitted
per day, or what percentage of workday is keyboarding permitted
(e.g. 50%). It should specify any quotas or job production
requirements.

3. Is there a physician’s report that states that the employee cannot
perform the essential functions of the job without exceeding work
restrictions or risk of additional injury. Employer cannot require a
release to return to work without any restrictions.

Robinson v. WCAB (1986) 51 CCC 559 (wd)
(Not being able to fulfill quota was grounds
for termination.)

4. Has a workplace environment study been performed in a timely,
good faith, interactive process with the employee and an expert to
determine if there are any effective reasonable accommodations that
can be made? This requires meeting with the employee.
Accommodations may be modifying workstation to be more
ergonomic, adjustable keyboards, special keyboards, floor mats, air
filters, etc. Accommodations could be leave, part-time work or
change in working hours.

5. Can reasonable accommodations be made so that the employee is
able to perform the essential functions without undue hardship
(expense) to the employer? Employer has burden to show
accommodations would produce an undue hardship to its operation.

6. Is there a physician’s report that states that the employee cannot
perform the essential functions of the job, as modified by the
reasonable accommodations, without exceeding work restrictions or
tisk of additional injury.

7. Is there an alternate vacant position available? Steps 2 through 6
should be repeated for the alternate job? The employee has a
preferential right to assignment to any job he or she is qualified and
able to perform provided it does not involve a substantial pay raise.
This does not supersede provisions of a collective bargaining
agreement or state civil service laws.

Spitzer v. The Good Guys, Inc. (2000) 80
CA4th 1376

## Chapter 125: Americans With Disabilities Act (Ada)

ADA does not preempt FEHA

Federal law prohibiting discrimination against “qualified individuals
with disabilities.” (QIWD)

42 USC§ 12101 et seq.

“Qualified” means: can perform the essential function of the job
with or without reasonable accommodations. Accommodation
cannot be a hardship to the employer.

EEOC Reg§1630.2

“Disability” means:

substantially limits a major life activity or

42 USC §12102(2)(A); 29 CFR
§1630.2(g)(1)(2002)

have a “record of” or

be “regarded as” having such impairment.

‘A worker unable to return to work for a year would have a “record
of” a substantial limiting impairment.

Work-related injuries do not always cause physical or mental
impairments severe enough to “substantially limit” a major life
activity and ADA wouldn’t apply. An impairment that only limits
employment activity is not substantial.

Toyota v. Williams US Supreme Court 67
CCC 60 (carpal tunnel was not substantial)

Where a worker substantially recovers from the injury but made a
workers’ comp claim - the worker would not be protected by ADA.

An employer may not inquire into an applicant’s workers’ comp
history before making a conditional offer of employment. (don’t
answer the question)

After making a conditional job offer, an employer may ask about a
person’s workers’ comp history in a medical inquiry or exam that is
required of all applicants in the same job category.

An employer may refuse to hire, or may discharge an individual who
is not currently able to perform a job without posing a significant
risk of substantial harm to the health or safety of the individual or
others, if the risk cannot be eliminated or reduced by reasonable
accommodations.

Applies to employers with 15 or more employees

Can pursue both ADA and WC remedies.

Wood v. County of Alameda 60 CCC 71, 23
CWCR 84

Employer cannot refuse to hire a person because he or she had used
drugs in the past, i.e. a recovering addict. But if an employee is
terminated for violating a company policy of failing a drug test the
employer is not required to rehire the person as long as the employer
has a policy of not rehiring persons who are terminated for violating
a company policy.

## Chapter 128: Social Security

Social Security Disability Income (SSDI)

Disability insurance benefits (DIB) title II is for disabled workers
who have contributed and are eligible.

Workers’ compensation attorney should consider having any worker
who is severely disabled apply for SSDI.

Disability is the inability to do substantial gainful activity by reason | 42 USC§§423(d)(1)(A), 1382c(a)(3)(A)
of any medically determinable physical or mental impairment,
which can be expected to result in death or has lasted, or can be
expected to last for 12 continuous months. And is unable to engage | 42 USC§§423(d)(2)(A), 13820(a)(3)(B)
in any other kind of substantial gainful work which exists in the
national economy in significant numbers. SGA is earning more than
$700 per month.

Five-step evaluation:

1. Has the person worked during the period of time that disability is CAAAments June 2000, page 16
claims? Can have trial work period of 3 to 9 months without
affecting benefits.

2. Are the claimant’s impairments severe? Impairments are severe if
they cause significant limitation in an individual’s ability to perform
work or work-related activity.

3. Do the claimant’s impairments meet any of the conditions
described in the Listing of Impairments? If so, the person is
presumptively disabled. Don’t need to do steps 4 and 5.

4. If the impairments do not meet a condition in the Listing of
Impairments, do the impairments prevent the claimant from
performing past relevant work? If so, ...

5. Do the impairments prevent the claimant from performing other
work in the national economy?

Procedures to Get SSDI

Worker to file application for SSDI. Form SSA-16-F6, 3369-F6, 827-OP1

Request for Reconsideration if denied within 60 days of denial Form SSA-561-U2, 3441-F6
notice.

File Request for Hearing if denied Form SSA-501-U5, HA-4486
Attorney usually involved at this stage.

Attorney’s fee is 25% of past due benefits up to $5300 Form SSA-1560, 1696-US
File Request for Review of Hearing if denied. Form HA-520-U6

There is 5 month waiting period for benefits.

Can get 12 months retroactive benefits.

MEDICARE is available after 2 years 5 months.

If combination of SS and WC benefits exceeds 80% of the average | 42 USC§424a, 424a(g), 20 CFR 404.408(h);
earnings (ACE) then SS benefits will be reduced up to age 65 http://www.ssa.gov/pubs/10018.html
(offset).

WC lump sum after attorney’s fee is considered to be payments
made for life expectancy or if less than100% consider payments to
be at PD rate.

Excluded are company sick & disability benefits death benefits,
home health aids, attorney’s fees and medical expenses.

Medicare Benefits

For effects on settlements see chapter on Compromise & Release

Eligible for Medicare if have received SSDI for 24 months or are
over the age of 65.

There are two categories of benefits. Part A requires no premium.
Part B coverage requires a premium of $45.50 per month. Part A
covers hospital, skilled nursing facility (SNF) care, home health care
including part time skilled nursing care, 80% of durable medical
equipment (wheelchairs, hospital beds, walkers), and hospice care.
Part B covers doctors, clinical lab, home health care, and outpatient
hospital.

LC§3201.7(a)(3)(C)

There are usually three major steps to the process. An ombudsman
first receives the complaint and tries to resolve the problem. If that
fails, the ombudsman assists the employee in filing a request for
mediation. Mediators are assigned from a list. If a party is not
satisfied with the mediation they may request arbitration. The
arbitrator may appoint a health care professional to assist in the
resolution of any medical issue. The arbitrator’s award may be
appealed to the WCAB by way of Petition for Reconsideration

The CBA generally provides for subpoena duces tecum s to be
issued by the arbitrator, discovery under LC §5710 (depositions) and
liens under LC §4903.

131.DISABILITY RETIREMENT — PUBLIC
EMPLOYEES

The Supreme Court affirmed CA’s reversal of Superior Court
judgment that police officer was entitled to disability retirement,
remanding case to Superior Court. The Supreme Court held that the
phrase “state service” means all forms of public agency service, not
merely employee’s last employer. Thus, on remand plaintiff must
show that he is unable to perform usual duties for this defendant and
usual duties of patrol officer for other California agencies. If
plaintiff does so, burden shifts to defendant to show that plaintiff is
capable of performing duties of patrol officer for other agencies and
that similar positions with other such agencies are available to him.

Nolan v. City of Anaheim (2004) 70 CCC 9
(Cal Supreme Court

IW alleged 132a against the Employer for delay in processing
disability application. Because IW initially resigned her
employment before seeking disability retirement, WCAB denied
132a claim because, at the time of the alleged 132a violation, IW
was not, technically, an Employee of defendant.

Sepulveda-Huth v. WCAB (2005) 70 CCC
152, 33 CWCR 38 (wd)

If disability retirement is denied due to lack of proof of disability,
the applicant is entitled to reinstatement, and to back pay if the
employee was previously ‘dismissed’ ‘for disability’, per Govt Code
Section 31725. However, an employer letter advising the worker to
stay away from work until his condition improves is not such a
‘dismissal for disability’.

Stephens v. County of Tulare (2006) 34
CWCR 137, 71 CCC 571 (Supreme Court)

In another decision in the area of County Employees Retirement
law, CA has concluded that an employee was not “dismissed” and
thus not entitled to reinstatement and back payCeven though the
employer advised it had no position available, placed employee on
unpaid leave, and offered VR. The CA claimed to be following the
decision in Stephens v. County of Tulare.

Kelly v. County of Los Angeles (2006) 71
CCC 934 (CA 2™ pub?)

Per Gov’t Code Section 31725, denial of disability retirement
requires reinstatement by Employer if employee had been
terminated b/c of PD, and requires payment of back pay retro to
dismissal date. In this case, employee failed to prove she had been
dismissed and employer failed to take action to clarify her status as
an employee; therefore, reinstatement denied.

Kelly v. County of Los Angeles_(2006) 34
CWCR 195,71 CCC 934 (CA 2™ - pub)

Per Gov’t Code Section 21153, if employer believes employee to be
disabled (from u&c duties), employer must apply for disability
retirement on behalf of employee (which also permits employee to
seek attorney’s fees). In this case, although county claimed
employee could work as deputy sheriff, county offered VR and
employee’s supervisor sent her home b/c there was no position for
her wii her restrictions. Thus, CA concluded that County believed
her to be disabled, requiring the County to either reinstate her
w/back pay or apply for disability retirement from employer.

Lazan v. County of Riverside (2006) 71 CCC
766, 34 CWCR 196 (CA 4" - pub)

County firefighter entitled to disability retirement under Gov Code
31720.5 based upon the heart presumption set forth in that section.

Pellerin v. Kern County Employees’
Retirement Assn (2006) 35 CWCR 64, 72
CCC 60 (CA 5" - pub)

No TD or VRMA after PERS disability retirement:
Firefighter not entitled to retro VRMA for period after effective
date of PERS disability retirement, based on Ritchie 59 CCC 243

Meiers v. WCAB (2006) 71 CCC 79 (CA 2™
- wd)

BUT, different result if service retirement: Sheriffs deputy entitled

OTHER CONSIDERATIONS NOT AT THE BOARD

County of Orange v. WCAB (2010) 75 CCC
1422 (CA 4""— wd)

BUT, Public safety PERS retiree subject to LC Section 4850 not
entitled to TTD after disability retirement per LC Section 4853.
Does not matter that initial retirement was based on service only; it
was later converted to disability retirement retroactively.

Follett v. WCAB (2010) 76 CCC 41 (CA i
wd)

Voluntary service retirement after 31 years as deputy sheriff did not
bar receipt of TD for period of TD after retirement; applicant
demonstrated intent to continue working by working part time jobs
both before and after retirement from sheriff's department

Kings County v. WCAB (Revious) (2011) 76
CCC 378 (CA 5" — wd)

County was obligated to apply for disability retirement on behalf of
county correctional officer under Gov’t Code Section 21153 b/c
evidence established that County believed plaintiff was disabled in
the form of w/c notices that plaintiff was QIW and entitled to VR
benefits.

Lewis v. County of Riverside (2007) 72 CCC
218 (CA 4" non pub)

LAPD officer’s disability pension can be reduced for work comp
benefits received w/o violating ADA, FEHA, etc.

Brown v. City of LA (2008) 36 CWCR 94
(9" circuit)

County must allow payment of accumulated sick leave to
industrially disabled police officers to be counted as “pensionable”
income b/c it allowed other workers to do so. Differential treatment
would violate Sections 132a and 4850.

LA County Prof Peace Officers Assn v.
County of LA (2008) 36 CWCR 172 (CA 2"

pub)

Denial of disability retirement upheld because applicant had not yet
undergone recommended pain program and thus not yet at MMI.

Acosta v. Sacramento County Employees
Retirement System (2010) 75 CCC 109 (CA
3 — nonpub)

Disabled civil service correctional officer (not a public safety
officer) not entitled to a disability retirement if ER offers a lower
paying desk job within the department that applicant is capable of
performing.

Gonzalez v. Department of Corrections
(2011) 39 CWCR 188 (CA 4" — pub)

Termination of employment after public safety applicant taken off
work due to industrial injury did not preclude later finding of
entitlement to disability retirement.

Zimmon vy. City of San Bernardino (2011) 76
CCC 1065, 39 CWCR 260 (CA 4" — nonpub)

Medical evidence supporting disability retirement application under
“37 Act” must establish “substantial contribution” from work that is
“real & measurable”.

Valero v. Board of Retirement of Tulare
County (2012) 40 CWCR 85 (CA Sth — pub)

City’s admission of worker’s compensation liability does not require
same finding by retirement system; no collateral estoppel because
retirement board and city not in privity.

Crumlish v. Board of Administration of San
Diego County Retirement System (2012) 77
CCC 325, 40 CWCR 89 (CA 4th — nonpub)

Standard of review for superior court in disability retirement matters
is “independent judgment”, rather than ‘supported by substantial
evidence’.

Alberta v. Board of Retirement of Fresno
County (2013) 41 CWCR 29, 78 CCC 547
(CA 5th - pub)

See also: Superior court must exercise its
independent judgment in reviewing case
involving retirement board’s denial of
industrial disability retirement, and did so in
order Boarfd to grant the disability retirement.
Alberda v. Board of Retirement of Fresno
County ... [Alberda II] (2015) 43 CWCR 57
(CA 5" (nonpub)

OTHER CONSIDERATIONS NOT AT THE BOARD

Kirk v. Retirement Board of the City of San
Francisco (2013) 41CWCR 113, 78 CCC 585
(CA Ist - nonpub)

Service-connected disability retirement denied because of absence
of a “real & measurable” connection between applicant’s disabling
psychiatric condition and her accepted orthopedic injury.

Rashid v. Board of Retirement of Alameda
County (2013) 41 CWCR 217 (CA 1*—non
pub)

Disability retirement must be granted when “accommodation” was
offered after clamant left City service and was merely theoretical in
that no specific job offer was made or even described

Meyers v. Bd of Admin for Federated City
Employees (2014) 79 CCC 284, 42 CWCR 29
(CA 6"- pub)

Test for disability retirement of CHP officer based on ability to
perform usual officer’s duties; fact that applicant was public affairs
officer not relevant

Beckley v. Board of Admin of Cal PERS
(2013) 78 CCC 1283, 42 CWCR 32 (CA 1*-
pub)

Claimant entitled to disability retirement benefits after last day of
regular employment, not after TD ended. Clamant is not barred from

Porter v. Board of Retirement of Orange
County (2013) 78 CCC 1305, 42 CWCR 33
(CA 4". pub)

receiving TD at same time as disability pension payments.

Per SF charter, a firefighter not yet eligible for service retirement
who qualifies for disability retirement shall receive a disability
pension based on the percentage of his PD as determined by the
WCAB.

Oswald v. San Francisco City and County
Employees Retirement System (2013) 79 CCC
52 (CA 1*- non pub)

Following denial of disability retirement pension, superior court
must exercise its independent judgment of the evidence; it should
not defer to the administrative fact finder or accept its findings
whenever substantial evidence supports them.

Rodriguez v. City of Santa Cruz (2014) 79
CCC 844 (CA 6" - pub?)

Proper standard of review for trial court reviewing Bd=s decision
was to use its independent judgement; trial court=s decision
reversing retirement board’s denial of service-connected disability
retirement was correct.

Fulkes v. Bd of Retirement of Tulare County
(2014) 79 CCC 1013 (CA 5" - non pub)

Applicant is not entitled to prejudgment interest on retroactive
disability retirement award for the period when his right to receive
benefits had not vested.

Flethez v. San Bernadino County Employees
Retirement Ass'n (2015) 43 CWCR 85 (CA
4"-pub)

Interest payable on retroactive disability retirement benefits due
from date Board incorrectly denied disability retirement, not from
the date last worked or date of application.

Flethez v. San Bernadino County Employees
Retirement Ass'n (2017) 45 CWCR 29 (Cal
Supreme Court)

Payment for unused sick & vacation upon retirement from police
department is reportable income for federal tax purposes because not
paid contemporaneous with 4850 pay or TD even though earned
during such periods of disability

Speer v. Commissioner of IRS (2015) 80 CCC
444 (US Tax Court)

Public Safety officer entitled to TD after PERS service retirement,
assuming applicant had not intended to remove himself from the
labor market.

City of Gardena v. WCAB (Funke) (2015) 80
CCC 1060 (CA 2"'— wd)

Although this firefighter of 30 years tenure established entitlement
to the cancer presumption in Gov’t Code Section 31720.6 (b), it was
rebutted by the Retirement Board’s evidence that the carcinogens to
which he was exposed were not reasonably linked to his prostate
cancer (Dr. Allems retained by the Bd).

Haskins v. Kern County Employees
Retirement Association (2015) 80 CCC 1151
(CA 5""—nonpub)

Public safety disability retiree is entitled to reinstatement without
conditions; after reinstatement, employer may evaluate fitness for
duty. [5 years of back pay awarded].

California Department of Justice v. Board of
Admin of CalPERS (2015) 81 CCC 1, 43
CWCR 273 (CA 2™- pub)

Advance pension benefits are “compensation” per Section 4850.4;
thus WCAB has jurisdiction to assess penalty for delayed payment.

Gage v. WCAB (2016) 81 CCC 1127, 44
CWCR 265 (CA 3d-nonpub)
